Use Data Vault D1, D4 and D9, then Source Annex A: Sessions 25-26.

## MODULE 16 — DOCTRINE EVOLUTION, CURRENT ANCHORS, PYQ DEMANDS AND ANSWER BUILDING

### WHAT ARE WE LEARNING?

**How do we convert a wide legal syllabus into a precise Prelims choice and a balanced GS-II answer?**

### TEACHER EXPLAINS

> **TEACHER EXPLAINS:** Do not write Fundamental Rights as a case-name parade. Each case should perform one job in a doctrinal evolution. For Article 21, use Gopalan → Cooper → Maneka → named dignity applications → Puttaswamy proportionality. For equality, use classification → Royappa anti-arbitrariness → enabling reservation clauses → Indra Sawhney controls → Janhit/Davinder current refinement. For speech, use 1950 cases → First Amendment → Kedar Nath incitement discipline → Shreya Singhal digital vagueness → Anuradha Bhasin shutdown procedure → Electoral Bonds political information.

Three general ideas add depth when supported. **Proportionality** asks whether a lawful measure pursuing a legitimate aim is suitably connected, necessary and balanced with safeguards. **Constitutional morality** protects the Constitution's liberty, equality and fraternity commitments against social domination, but remains tied to text and structure. **Transformative constitutionalism** explains why Part III attacks caste, gender and status hierarchies and sometimes requires positive protection, not merely State abstention.

Current anchors must be dated. The active official-source audit through 21 August 2026 retains five controlled propositions: Electoral Bonds protects political-funding information; *Davinder Singh* permits evidence-based SC sub-classification; *Property Owners* preserves original Article 31C and a contextual private-resource inquiry; DPDP commencement is phased; BNS section 152 is operative and under a notified constitutional challenge without a located final merits disposition. This edition does not extend the control date or invent later outcomes.

Prelims elimination begins with exact words: citizen/person, tax/fee, internal/foreign travel, automatic/order-based Emergency effect, preventive-detention period, prohibition/certiorari, title/award, direct/indirect horizontality. Mains answers begin with the claimant and State action, not a generic history of Part III.

### SIMPLE EXAMPLE / ANALOGY

Cases are stepping stones across a river. Naming every stone does not cross the river. A good answer shows how one stone changes the route: Gopalan's narrow channel, Cooper's effect test, Maneka's fair procedure and Puttaswamy's privacy test.

### VISUAL FIRST

![A marks-oriented answer identifies the claimant, Article, limit, case and remedy.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/16_mains_answer_spine.png)

```text
10-MARK SPINE
claimant/respondent -> Article -> protected interest -> exact limit
-> one controlling case -> remedy -> qualified verdict

15/20-MARK SPINE
architecture -> doctrine evolution -> clause-level application
-> competing right/DPSP -> proportionality -> remedy
-> current dated anchor -> balanced conclusion
```

### NOW ADD THE EXACT LAW

> **NOW ADD THE EXACT LAW:** The active source verifies 21 routed PYQs: six Mains questions, thirteen 2018-2023 Prelims routes labelled **INFERRED ANSWER - NOT OFFICIALLY VERIFIED**, the 2024 privacy question with an official local key, and the 2026 Article 13/custom question with a provisional non-official key. Exact stems, keys, explanations, original practice and remedials remain in Data Vault D8 and Source Annex A/B.

### COMMON CONFUSION

> **COMMON CONFUSION:** “More case names automatically produce more marks, and every current case should be described as finally settled.” → **Correct position:** use a short doctrine chain in which every holding proves a claim and carries a limit. Date-bound current status must state what the official record does and does not establish.

### WHAT MUST I REMEMBER?

1. Use cases as doctrine evolution, not decoration.
2. Every case sentence should contain holding + use + limit.
3. Proportionality is structured justification, not the word “balance.”
4. Constitutional morality remains tied to constitutional text and values.
5. Current claims stop at the verified control date.
6. Prelims depends on close wording and exception discipline.
7. Mains begins with claimant, State action, Article and directive.
8. End with a reasoned verdict, not an automatic “balance is needed.”

### CHECK YOUR UNDERSTANDING

1. How would you turn Gopalan, Cooper, Maneka and Puttaswamy into one doctrine-evolution paragraph?
2. What information must accompany a current case before it is safe to use in a Mains answer?

### EXAM USE

> **EXAM USE:** **Universal answer framework:** direct thesis → exact text and beneficiary → doctrine evolution → named evidence showing what it proves → competing value/limit → proportionality and procedure → Article 32/226 remedy → dated current anchor → graded verdict. See the exact PYQ demand map before practising.

### IF YOU WANT THE FULL DETAIL

Use Data Vault D2-D3, D7-D12, then Source Annex A/B for every complete solution.
## FINAL ANSWERS — CHECK YOUR UNDERSTANDING

### Module 1 answers

1. Beneficiary asks who owns the right; respondent asks who is constitutionally bound. A non-citizen may own Article 14, while a private body may fail Article 12 but still be directly bound by Article 23 or face Article 226 for a public duty.
2. Articles 15(2), 17, 23 and 24.

### Module 2 answers

1. Article 12 status and Article 226 public-duty reach are different inquiries. A body may lack pervasive governmental domination yet owe a public legal duty enforceable by a High Court.
2. Severability removes an unconstitutional portion when the valid remainder can stand. Eclipse primarily makes a conflicting pre-Constitution rule dormant against the protected claim, allowing possible revival if the constitutional obstacle is validly removed.

### Module 3 answers

1. Yes. An intelligible differentia fails if it has no rational nexus with the law's object.
2. It allowed Article 14 to invalidate capricious, unguided or unfair State action even when classic group comparison was incomplete.

### Module 4 answers

1. Article 15(3) authorises special provisions for women and children across the Article 15 field; Article 16(4) is a public-employment provision for an inadequately represented backward class. Their beneficiaries, field and conditions differ.
2. It permitted evidence-based sub-classification within Scheduled Castes without changing the Article 341 list. It did not require every State to create a sub-quota.

### Module 5 answers

1. Speech restrictions must fit the closed grounds in Article 19(2). “General public interest” belongs to Articles 19(5) and 19(6), not to speech as a freestanding ground.
2. Article 21 protects travel abroad through personal liberty and fair procedure. Article 19(1)(d) concerns movement throughout the territory of India.

### Module 6 answers

1. Discussion and advocacy normally remain protected; restriction becomes constitutionally stronger at incitement, where expression has the required close connection to a listed Article 19(2) harm.
2. Section 152 has differently framed ingredients and is operative. The active audit located a constitutional-challenge notice but no final merits decision through 21 August 2026.

### Module 7 answers

1. Article 20(3) targets compelled testimonial communication by an accused. Fingerprints and other physical identifying material are analytically distinct, though Article 21 and statutory safeguards still apply.
2. Gopalan accepted narrow lawful procedure and compartmentalised rights. Maneka required fair, just and reasonable procedure and linked Articles 14, 19 and 21.

### Module 8 answers

1. A legitimate aim is only one step. The State must also show legal authority, suitable connection, necessity, balance and adequate safeguards.
2. Article 21A and the RTE Act create education obligations, but Pramati protects Article 30 minority institutions from an application that would destroy their constitutional autonomy.

### Module 9 answers

1. A magistrate independently authorises ordinary post-arrest custody. An Advisory Board reviews preventive detention under a special constitutional regime; it is not the ordinary criminal-production safeguard.
2. The 44th Amendment enacted a two-month substitution, but it was not commenced. The operative no-Advisory-Board period remains three months.

### Module 10 answers

1. PUDR treated severe economic compulsion and payment below the statutory minimum as capable of making labour “forced” under Article 23.
2. Article 24 text covers children below fourteen in factories, mines and hazardous employment. Statute creates a broader child/adolescent labour framework with its own definitions and exceptions.

### Module 11 answers

1. Article 25 protects every person's conscience and profession/practice/propagation; Article 26 protects a religious denomination or section in institutional and religious affairs.
2. Constitutional morality derives authority from constitutional text, structure and liberty-equality-fraternity commitments. Detached from them, it becomes an undefined personal preference rather than a legal test.

### Module 12 answers

1. Article 29 speaks of sections of citizens and citizen admissions; Article 30 speaks of all religious or linguistic minorities. The beneficiary language is not identical.
2. Standards, qualifications, transparency and anti-exploitation rules may regulate an institution. A rule becomes unconstitutional when it takes over or destroys the minority management's core and identity.

### Module 13 answers

1. Prohibition, because it prevents the tribunal from completing action outside jurisdiction. Certiorari ordinarily quashes a completed defective order.
2. Article 226 reaches Fundamental Rights and other legal rights and has wider public-duty scope. Article 32 remains a guaranteed Fundamental Right for Supreme Court enforcement of Part III.

### Module 14 answers

1. I.R. Coelho permits basic-structure review of post-24 April 1973 Ninth Schedule insertions by examining their rights impact.
2. The former protection was inside Part III and included Article 19(1)(f)/31 remedies. Article 300A is a constitutional right of every person outside Part III requiring authority of law.

### Module 15 answers

1. Article 359 changes access to court for enforcement of named rights during the order; it does not amend the constitutional text or permanently extinguish the right.
2. Armed rebellion. Article 358 is limited to Emergencies based on war or external aggression.

### Module 16 answers

1. Gopalan used narrow legal procedure and rights silos; Cooper tested the effect of State action; Maneka required fair, just and reasonable procedure across Articles 14, 19 and 21; Puttaswamy applied that dignity-based liberty to bodily, decisional and informational privacy through structured justification.
2. State the official decision or notification, date, precise holding/operation, control date, later procedural status if verified, and what no located official source permits you to claim.

### D2. Doctrine and test matrix

| Doctrine/test | Operational rule | Leading anchors | Qualification |
|---|---|---|---|
| State/instrumentality | financial + functional + administrative domination; cumulative control | R.D. Shetty; Ajay Hasia; Pradeep Kumar Biswas | mere regulation/public importance is not conclusive |
| Public-duty writ | body may face Article 226 for a public duty without being Article 12 State | Andi Mukta; Zee Telefilms distinction | do not collapse into Article 12 |
| Severability | remove invalid part if remainder is workable and intended | R.M.D. Chamarbaugwala | void only to extent |
| Eclipse | inconsistent pre-Constitution law becomes dormant against protected claim | Bhikaji Narain Dhakras | not automatic repeal; use carefully for post-Constitution law |
| Waiver | person generally cannot validate unconstitutional State action by consent | Basheshar Nath | public constitutional guarantee |
| Prospective overruling | new constitutional rule may operate prospectively | Golaknath | case-specific judicial technique |
| Reasonable classification | intelligible differentia + rational nexus | Ram Krishna Dalmia | both limbs required |
| Anti-arbitrariness | capricious/unguided State action violates equality | E.P. Royappa; Maneka Gandhi | do not replace every textual test with a slogan |
| Manifest arbitrariness | legislation may fail for capricious, irrational determining principle | Shayara Bano opinions | state fragmented context precisely |
| Proportionality | law + legitimate aim + suitability + necessity + balance + safeguards | Modern Dental College; Puttaswamy; Anuradha Bhasin | formulations vary; explain steps |
| Incitement/vagueness | discussion/advocacy protected; incitement and close harm matter | Shreya Singhal; Kedar Nath | closed Article 19(2) grounds still control |
| Essential Religious Practices | identify protected religious matter versus secular/regulable activity | Shirur Mutt | judicial-theology and equality criticisms |
| Constitutional morality | liberty/equality/fraternity discipline social and public power | Navtej; Joseph Shine | not free-floating personal morality |
| Basic structure | amendment cannot destroy foundational constitutional identity | Kesavananda; Minerva Mills | full doctrine belongs to Topic 10 |
| Ninth Schedule review | post-24 April 1973 entry tested for basic-structure damage | Waman Rao; I.R. Coelho | not blanket invalidity |
| Positive obligation | State may need prevention/remedy against private domination | Vishaka; D.K. Basu line | does not make every private actor State |

| Year | Amendment/event | Fundamental-Rights effect |
|---|---|---|
| 1951 | First Amendment | inserted 15(4), 31A, 31B/Ninth Schedule; recast 19(2) with reasonable restrictions and added grounds |
| 1955 | Fourth Amendment | strengthened property-acquisition and 31A/31B framework |
| 1963 | Sixteenth Amendment | added sovereignty and integrity grounds to 19(2)-(4) and related oath controls |
| 1971 | Twenty-fourth Amendment | affirmed Parliament's amendment power; inserted Articles 13(4) and 368 changes |

| 1972 | Twenty-ninth Amendment | added Kerala land-reform laws to Ninth Schedule |
| 1976 | Forty-second Amendment | attempted to extend 31C to all DPSPs; inserted 31D |
| 1977 | Forty-third Amendment | repealed Article 31D |
| 1978 | Forty-fourth Amendment | removed property from Part III; inserted 300A; narrowed 358; excluded 20/21 from 359; uncommenced detention change |
| 1995 | Seventy-seventh Amendment | inserted 16(4A) promotion reservation for SC/ST |
| 2000 | Eighty-first Amendment | inserted 16(4B) backlog vacancies |
| 2000 | Eighty-second Amendment | added Article 335 proviso for qualifying-mark/standard relaxation |
| 2001 | Eighty-fifth Amendment | consequential seniority in promotion framework |

| 2005 | Ninety-third Amendment | inserted Article 15(5) educational special-provision power |
| 2011 | Ninety-seventh Amendment | added cooperative societies to Article 19(1)(c) |
| 2019 | One Hundred and Third Amendment | inserted 15(6) and 16(6) EWS provisions |
| 2021 | One Hundred and Fifth Amendment | restored States' power to identify SEBCs for their purposes |

**Property/Emergency rapid chain**

```text
PROPERTY: 19(1)(f)+31 -> 31A/B -> 31C -> 44th Amendment -> Article 300A
EMERGENCY: ADM Jabalpur failure -> 44th Amendment -> 358 narrowed + 20/21 protected from 359
RESERVATION: 15(4) -> Indra Sawhney -> 77/81/82/85 -> 93rd -> 103rd -> Davinder Singh
```

### D5. Citizen-only, person-rights and group-rights matrix

| Provision | Beneficiary | Respondent/reach |
|---|---|---|
| 14 | any person | primarily State; anti-arbitrariness |
| 15(1) | citizen | State |
| 15(2) | citizen | State and specified private access discrimination |
| 16 | citizen | State/public employment |
| 17 | everyone protected against untouchability | State and private actors |
| 18 | citizens/persons in clause-specific form | State/citizen title controls |
| 19 | citizen | State |
| 20 | person | State criminal process |
| 21 | person | State plus positive-protection duties |
| 21A | children aged 6-14 | State; statutory school obligations with Article 30 boundary |
| 22 | person subject to textual exceptions | State arrest/detention |
| 23 | person | State and private actors |
| 24 | child below 14 | State and private employers |
| 25 | person | State; conscience/religion |
| 26 | religious denomination or section | State |
| 27 | person | State taxation |
| 28 | person/student/guardian in clause context | covered educational institutions |
| 29 | section of citizens / citizen | State and State-maintained/aided admissions |
| 30 | religious or linguistic minority | State; institution regulation |
| 32 | holder of a Fundamental Right | Supreme Court remedy |

### D6. Writs and Article 32/226 retrieval table

| Writ | Purpose | Typical respondent/defect | Trap/limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Habeas corpus | produce detained person and test legality | public or private unlawful detention | not issued when custody is demonstrably lawful under valid authority |
| Mandamus | compel performance of public duty | public authority or body owing public duty | not a general order enforcing purely private contract |
| Prohibition | stop proceedings before completion | court/tribunal acting outside jurisdiction | preventive, not for a completed order |
| Certiorari | quash completed defective order | court/tribunal/public decision with jurisdictional/legal error | corrective; not an appeal on every factual disagreement |
| Quo warranto | test authority to hold public office | holder of substantive public office | focuses on legal qualification, not personal injury |

**Court comparison:** Article 32 is guaranteed for Supreme Court enforcement of Fundamental Rights. Article 226 is wider for Fundamental Rights and other legal rights, with broader public-duty and territorial operation.

### D9. Exceptions and edge cases

| Trap | Precise correction |
|---|---|
| Article 30 beneficiary | Text says all religious or linguistic minorities; do not mechanically label it citizen-only. |
| Article 29 beneficiary | Not minority-only; it protects sections of citizens and citizen admission rights. |
| Horizontal reach | 15(2), 17, 23 and 24 directly reach specified private conduct; other claims may use positive duty/statute/public-duty writ. |
| Article 12 | Public function alone is not always conclusive; apply cumulative government domination. |
| Article 13 eclipse | Primarily a pre-Constitution dormant-law doctrine; do not call every invalid post-Constitution law eclipsed. |
| Classification | An intelligible differentia without rational nexus still fails. |
| Reservation ceiling | Fifty per cent is an ordinary doctrinal ceiling with exceptional reasoning, not an unqualified mathematical statement. |
| Davinder Singh | Permits evidence-based sub-classification; does not mandate it or alter Article 341 list. |
| Article 18 | Military/academic distinctions and properly used national awards are not prohibited titles. |
| Travel | Internal movement/residence = Article 19; travel abroad = Article 21. |
| Shreya Singhal | 66A struck; 69A blocking framework not struck; section 79 read down. |
| Anuradha Bhasin | Protected internet as medium and shutdown procedure; no broad standalone internet FR holding. |
| Article 20(2) | Requires prosecution and punishment for same offence; departmental/administrative action may differ. |
| Article 20(3) | Testimonial compulsion differs from physical evidence; other safeguards remain. |
| Derived Article 21 rights | Name the holding and limit; do not convert every aspiration into an absolute entitlement. |
| Article 22 period | Operative no-Advisory-Board period is three months; 44th Amendment two-month substitution uncommenced. |
| Article 24 | Constitutional below-14 hazardous-employment text differs from wider statute. |
| Article 27 | Bar concerns specified tax appropriation, not every regulatory fee. |
| Propagation | Does not protect force, fraud or allurement conversion. |
| Minority administration | No right to maladminister; valid standards cannot destroy identity/control. |
| Writ sequence | Prohibition prevents; certiorari quashes completed action. |
| Article 31B | Ninth Schedule not absolutely immune after I.R. Coelho. |
| Article 31C | Surviving protection tied to Article 39(b)/(c), not all DPSPs. |
| Article 300A | Constitutional right of every person, not present FR or citizen-only right. |
| Article 358 | Article 19 only; war/external aggression Emergency. |
| Article 359 | Order-based enforcement suspension; Articles 20-21 excluded. |
| Current claims | Stop at 21 Aug 2026 audit; do not invent later final judgments or full DPDP commencement. |

### D10. Glossary

| Term | Meaning for this topic |
|---|---|
| Arbitrariness | capricious or unreasoned State action lacking fair determining principle |
| Authority of law | deprivation must be supported by valid enacted law, not mere executive order |
| Begar | forced unpaid labour |
| Citizen-only right | a guarantee whose text gives the claim to citizens |
| Constitutional morality | fidelity to constitutional liberty, equality, fraternity, text and structure |
| Differentia | basis used to separate one class from another |
| Eclipse | dormancy of inconsistent law against the protected constitutional claim |
| ERP | Essential Religious Practices doctrine distinguishing protected religious matters from regulable secular activity |
| Horizontal application | right operating against private conduct directly or through positive/statutory duties |
| Instrumentality | entity constitutionally treated as State because government domination is sufficiently deep |
| Intelligible | understandable and non-arbitrary |
| Manifest arbitrariness | extreme caprice/irrationality in a legislative determining principle |
| Nexus | rational connection between classification and law's object |
| Positive obligation | State duty to prevent, protect, investigate or remedy rights harm |
| Preventive detention | detention aimed at anticipated future conduct rather than punishment for proved offence |
| Principled distance | Indian secular engagement or separation calibrated to equal citizenship and reform |
| Proportionality | structured test of legality, aim, suitability, necessity, balance and safeguards |
| Severability | removal of unconstitutional part while valid independent remainder survives |
| State action | conduct attributable to constitutionally bound public power |
| Transformative constitutionalism | use of constitutional guarantees to dismantle entrenched status hierarchy |
| Vertical application | right enforced against the State |
| Waiver | intentional surrender; generally unavailable to validate unconstitutional State action |

### D11. Final consolidated register notes

#### Part III operating map

- Gatekeepers: Articles 12-13.
- Equality: Articles 14-18.
- Freedom and personal protection: Articles 19-22.
- Exploitation: Articles 23-24.
- Religion: Articles 25-28.
- Culture/education: Articles 29-30.
- Remedy: Article 32.
- Savings/control: Articles 31A-31C and 33-35.
- Emergency effects: Articles 358-359.
- Property today: Article 300A, every person, authority of law.

#### Beneficiary and reach drill

- Citizen-specific: Articles 15, 16, 19 and 29.
- Person-protective anchors: Articles 14, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24, 25 and 27; read every clause exactly.
- Group/institution rights: Articles 26 and 30.
- Direct specified horizontal reach: 15(2), 17, 23, 24.
- Public-duty route: Article 226 may reach a non-State body.
- Positive duty: State protection may be required without converting every private actor into State.

#### Article 12-13 decision drill

- Article 12: cumulative financial, functional and administrative domination.
- Mere regulation or importance is not enough.
- Article 13: pre-law inconsistency / post-law prohibition / void to extent.
- Doctrines: severability, eclipse, non-waiver, prospective overruling.
- Amendment: Article 368 + basic structure, not ordinary Article 13 alone.

#### Equality and reservation control

- Article 14: differentia + nexus + anti-arbitrariness.
- Articles 15/16: separate grounds, fields and enabling clauses.
- Indra Sawhney: OBC creamy layer, ordinary ceiling, no 16(4) promotion.
- Promotion amendments: 77th/81st/82nd/85th; Nagaraj/Jarnail controls.
- EWS: 103rd Amendment upheld 3:2 in Janhit.
- Davinder: evidence-based SC sub-classification permitted, not mandated.
- Article 17 = caste untouchability; Article 18 = no titles, awards qualified.

#### Article 19 exact-law drill

- Speech 19(2): sovereignty/integrity; State security; friendly relations; public order; decency/morality; contempt; defamation; incitement.
- Assembly 19(3): sovereignty/integrity; public order.
- Association 19(4): sovereignty/integrity; public order; morality.
- Movement/residence 19(5): general public; Scheduled Tribe protection.
- Profession/trade 19(6): general public; qualifications; State monopoly.
- Foreign travel = Article 21.
- Shreya: 66A void, 69A survives, 79 read down.
- Anuradha: publish/review/proportionality; no standalone internet-right overclaim.

#### Criminal process, dignity and privacy

- Article 20(1): no retrospective offence/greater penalty.
- 20(2): prosecution + punishment + same offence.
- 20(3): accused + compulsion + testimonial communication.
- Article 21 arc: Gopalan -> Cooper -> Maneka.
- Named rights: dignity, legal aid, speedy trial, livelihood with limits, custodial safeguards, privacy and choice.
- Privacy: law + aim + proportionality + safeguards.
- DNA: strong foundation/eminent need + less-intrusive proof + child welfare + statutory presumption.
- DPDP: phased commencement only through audit date.

#### Education, detention and exploitation

- Article 21A: 6-14; RTE Act; Pramati minority boundary.
- Ordinary arrest: grounds + lawyer + magistrate within 24 hours.
- Preventive detention: separate regime; operative no-Board period 3 months.
- Article 23: trafficking, begar, economic forced labour; private reach.
- Article 24: below 14 / factory, mine, hazardous employment; statute broader.

#### Religion and minority institutions

- 25: persons; conscience/profess/practise/propagate; limits + reform.
- 26: denomination; religious affairs and property; public order/morality/health.
- ERP: Shirur Mutt foundation; judicial-theology/equality critique.
- 27: specified tax, not every fee.
- 28: funding/administration/aid/consent categories.
- 29: not minority-only; sections of citizens and admission rule.
- 30: religious/linguistic minorities; administration, not maladministration.

#### Remedies, savings and Emergency

- 32 = Supreme Court + FR; 226 = High Court + FR/other legal rights.
- Writs: detention / duty / threatened excess / completed error / public office.
- 31A, 31B, 31C are distinct.
- Coelho: post-1973 Ninth Schedule basic-structure review.
- Property Owners: original 31C survives; not every private asset.
- 33 Parliament; 34 martial-law indemnity; 35 Parliament-only fields.
- 358 automatic Article 19 under war/external aggression.
- 359 order-based enforcement suspension; never Articles 20-21.

#### Current and PYQ answer control

- Current control date: 21 August 2026.
- Electoral Bonds: political-funding information and equality/transparency.
- Davinder: permission, evidence, Article 341 unchanged.
- Property Owners: 31C survives, contextual resource inquiry.
- BNS 152: distinct text, operative, challenge notice, no located final merits decision.
- DPDP: only notified tranche operative by control date.
- Inferred/provisional Prelims keys remain labelled non-official.
- Every Mains paragraph: claim -> exact Article/case -> what it proves -> limit -> remedy/verdict.

```ascii-master
Union/State organs + local authorities + other authorities

  -> Pradeep Kumar Biswas: cumulative instrumentality inquiry

ARTICLE 13 INQUIRY
pre-Constitution inconsistent law -> void to extent of inconsistency
post-Constitution abridging law  -> prohibited and void to that extent
"law" includes ordinance, rule, regulation, notification, custom and usage

DOCTRINES
severability | eclipse | waiver generally unavailable | prospective overruling

AMENDMENTS: Article 13(4) + Article 368 -> Kesavananda basic-structure review.
```

```ascii-master
  77th/81st/82nd/85th -> promotion, backlog, qualification and seniority tools
```

```ascii-master
SPEECH GROUNDS UNDER 19(2)

decency/morality | contempt | defamation | incitement

ANCHORS

Shreya Singhal: 66A void; advocacy differs from incitement
```

```ascii-master
  +-- no retrospective criminal offence or enhanced penalty
```

```ascii-master
ARTICLES 25-28
individual conscience and profession, practice and propagation

limits: public order, morality, health and other FRs

ARTICLES 29-30
29(1): any section of citizens with distinct language, script or culture
29(2): admission non-discrimination in covered institutions
```

#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 7/10: Article 32: writ remedies and the enforcement ladder

```ascii-master
RIGHT VIOLATED
  +-- ARTICLE 32 -> SUPREME COURT for Fundamental Rights
  +-- ARTICLE 226 -> HIGH COURT for FRs and other legal rights
        |
WRIT CHOICE
HABEAS CORPUS   produce detained person; test detention
MANDAMUS        compel public duty
PROHIBITION     stop jurisdictional excess before completion
CERTIORARI      quash completed order for jurisdictional or legal error
QUO WARRANTO    test authority to hold public office

Article 32 is itself a Fundamental Right; legal fit still matters.
Article 226 is territorially and remedially wider, not constitutionally inferior.
```

#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 9/10: Article 358 and 359: emergency effects are not the same

```ascii-master
ARTICLE 358                         ARTICLE 359
automatic operation                Presidential order required
Article 19 only                     specified rights named in order
war/external-aggression Emergency   Emergency while order operates
changes State's Article 19 limit    suspends enforcement, not text
post-44th nexus conditions apply    Articles 20 and 21 cannot be included

ARTICLES 33-35
Parliament may tailor FRs for forces and specified services;
may indemnify martial-law acts and has exclusive competence over listed matters.

MARTIAL LAW != NATIONAL EMERGENCY
local exceptional coercive control differs from constitutional proclamation.

No rule says all Fundamental Rights automatically disappear in Emergency.
```

#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 10/10: Exact 10/15-mark architecture: liberty, limitation and remedy

```ascii-master
10 MARKS / 150 WORDS
1. Identify claimant, State action and exact Article.
2. State citizen/person reach, vertical/horizontal effect and duty.
3. Apply protected interest -> listed limit -> proportionality.
4. Add one controlling case and Article 32 or 226 remedy.
5. Conclude on validity with a narrow qualification.

15 MARKS / 250 WORDS
1. Frame Part III through gatekeepers, rights, limits, remedies and savings.
2. Explain the Article family with clause-level accuracy.
3. Apply Article 14 fairness and Article 19/21 proportionality where relevant.
4. Integrate rights-DPSP harmony through Minerva Mills.
5. Test amendment/immunity through Kesavananda and I.R. Coelho.
6. Distinguish Article 358 from 359 if Emergency is involved.
7. Conclude: governance requires legality, justification and remedy.
```

- [LIMIT] No Waqf, Places of Worship or Sabarimala final-status claim is made without a securely located official controlling decision.
#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Answer-line control register

The following sentences appear unchanged in the relevant teaching stage, the learning PDF and the separate flowchart companion.

1. **ARCHITECTURE:** Part III is a constitutional operating system, not a bare catalogue: Articles 12-13 identify bound power and invalid law, Articles 14-30 secure six present rights, Article 32 supplies the remedy, and Articles 31A-35 plus 358-359 control exceptional protection or enforcement.
2. **BENEFICIARIES / HORIZONTALITY:** Fundamental-right analysis must separate beneficiary from respondent: some guarantees belong only to citizens, many protect every person, and Articles 15(2), 17, 23 and 24 directly reach specified private conduct while positive duties may require State protection.
3. **QUALIFIED RIGHTS / PROPORTIONALITY:** Indian Fundamental Rights are qualified but not defeasible at executive will: every restriction must rest on valid law, fit the exact constitutional ground, pursue a legitimate aim and remain rational, necessary, balanced and procedurally safeguarded.
4. **PROPERTY / EMERGENCY CORE:** The Forty-fourth Amendment moved property from Part III to the constitutional guarantee in Article 300A and made Articles 20 and 21 non-derogable from an Article 359 enforcement order.
5. **ARTICLE 12:** Article 12 looks beyond legal form to governmental domination: an entity may be State when its financial, functional and administrative relationship reveals pervasive governmental control, while Article 226 may separately reach a non-State body performing a public duty.
6. **ARTICLE 13:** Article 13 converts rights into judicially enforceable limits by defining law broadly and using severability, eclipse and non-waiver, while constitutional amendments are reviewed through Article 368 and the basic-structure boundary.
7. **ARTICLE 14:** Article 14 combines equality before law with equal protection: it permits reasonable classification only through intelligible differentia and rational nexus, and independently invalidates arbitrary State action.
8. **ARTICLES 15-16:** Articles 15 and 16 move equality from non-discrimination to substantive opportunity by prohibiting exact grounds yet authorising carefully targeted special provisions and reservation through distinct enabling clauses.
9. **RESERVATION CONTROL:** Reservation is a technique of substantive equality, but its design remains evidence-based, reviewable and attentive to creamy-layer exclusion, representation, promotion conditions, backlog treatment, EWS text and administrative efficiency.
10. **ARTICLES 17-18:** Articles 17 and 18 attack inherited civic hierarchy horizontally and symbolically: untouchability is abolished and punishable, while titles are prohibited without invalidating military, academic or properly used national distinctions.
11. **ARTICLE 19:** Article 19 protects six citizen freedoms through clause-specific discipline: the State must identify the exact restriction clause and closed ground, after which reasonableness and proportionality test the means.
12. **ARTICLE 20:** Article 20 creates three person-protective criminal shields against retrospective penal liability, repeated prosecution-and-punishment for the same offence, and compelled testimonial self-incrimination.
13. **ARTICLE 21:** Article 21 became a dignity charter when Maneka Gandhi required fair, just and reasonable procedure and linked liberty with Articles 14 and 19, enabling named protections without turning every policy aspiration into an unlimited entitlement.
14. **ARTICLE 21A:** Article 21A constitutionalises free and compulsory education for children aged six to fourteen, implemented by the RTE Act while preserving the distinct Article 30 position of minority institutions.
15. **ARTICLE 22:** Article 22 separates ordinary arrest from preventive detention: the first carries grounds, counsel and twenty-four-hour production safeguards, while the second follows a distinct regime whose current no-Advisory-Board ceiling remains three months.
16. **ARTICLES 23-24:** Articles 23-24 constitutionalise labour dignity against State and private exploitation: trafficking, begar (forced unpaid labour), economically compelled forced labour and prohibited child labour attract both rights remedies and statutory enforcement.
17. **ARTICLES 25-28:** Indian religious freedom protects conscience and denominational autonomy while permitting public-order, health, morality, secular-regulation and social-reform controls; the governing model is principled distance, not either theocracy or an absolute wall.
18. **ARTICLES 29-30:** Articles 29-30 protect cultural conservation, non-discriminatory admission and minority educational autonomy, but Article 29 is not minority-exclusive and Article 30 protects administration rather than maladministration.
19. **ARTICLE 32 / WRITS:** Article 32 makes the Supreme Court a guaranteed Fundamental-Rights forum, while Article 226 is wider in subject matter and public-duty reach; the correct writ follows the defect—detention, unperformed duty, threatened excess, defective order or unlawful public office.
20. **SAVING / CONTROL PROVISIONS:** Articles 31A-31C, 33-35 create defined constitutional controls rather than rights-free zones: their field, purpose and legislative competence remain text-bound and subject to basic-structure review where applicable.
21. **EMERGENCY:** Article 358 automatically displaces Article 19 constraints only during war or external-aggression Emergency within its conditions, whereas Article 359 requires a Presidential order suspending court enforcement of specified rights and can never include Articles 20 or 21.
22. **CURRENT CASES:** Current Part III analysis must be date-bounded: Electoral Bonds protects political-funding information, Davinder Singh permits evidence-based Scheduled Caste sub-classification, Property Owners preserves original Article 31C, DPDP commencement is phased, and BNS section 152 remains under a notified constitutional challenge without a located final merits decision.
23. **FINAL VERDICT:** Fundamental Rights are neither absolute liberties nor revocable State gifts: they are transformative, judicially enforceable guarantees whose legitimacy lies in exact text, proportionate limitation, positive protection and a non-derogable rule-of-law core.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Package method, roadmap boundary and current-law control

- Source order followed: certified Core owner `Polity/basic/Fundamental-Rights.md` → separately labelled optional Advanced owner `Polity/advanced/07_Fundamental-Rights.md` → audited local PYQ ledgers, official/local papers and keys, and OCR-searchable M. Laxmikanth chapter → official constitutional, statutory and Supreme Court materials → Qdrant not used.
- [LIMIT] The Core learning session below independently secures the paper outcome. The optional Advanced section adds doctrinal depth; skipping it does not remove any essential Article, restriction ground, writ, saving clause, Emergency rule, routed PYQ or answer structure.
- [FACT] Part III contains Articles 12-35. The six present rights are equality, freedom, protection against exploitation, freedom of religion, cultural and educational rights, and constitutional remedies.
- [FACT] The Right to Property ceased to be a Fundamental Right through the Forty-fourth Amendment. Article 300A now protects every person against deprivation of property except by authority of law.
- [FACT] Fundamental Rights are qualified, not absolute. The Constitution itself specifies the beneficiaries, respondent, exceptions and restriction grounds; courts review whether the State stayed within those limits.
- [CURRENT] Legal status was checked through 21 August 2026 against located official Supreme Court, Gazette, MeitY, MHA and India Code materials. Bounded official-domain searches located no later decision altering Electoral Bonds, Davinder Singh or Property Owners. The package uses the notified DPDP commencement schedule and the official 28 August 2025 Section 152 notice order, but makes no unverified final-status claim about Waqf, Sabarimala or Places of Worship litigation.
- [CURRENT] *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Union of India*, 15 February 2024, invalidated the Electoral Bond Scheme and enabling non-disclosure architecture: voter information about political funding falls within Article 19(1)(a), while unlimited corporate funding and secrecy impaired political equality and transparency.
- [CURRENT] *State of Punjab v. Davinder Singh*, 1 August 2024, held that sub-classification within Scheduled Castes is constitutionally permissible. It permits evidence-based sub-classification; it does not compel every State to create a sub-quota.
- [CURRENT] *Property Owners Association v. State of Maharashtra*, 5 November 2024, confirmed that original Article 31C survives for laws genuinely giving effect to Article 39(b) or (c). Privately owned resources may in context qualify as material resources of the community, but not every private asset does.
- [CURRENT] The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 has operated from 1 July 2024. Section 152 is a successor provision concerning secession, armed rebellion, subversive or separatist activity and threats to sovereignty, unity and integrity. It is not accurately described as a simple renaming of IPC section 124A.
- [CURRENT] Gazette notification G.S.R. 843(E), 13 November 2025 commenced Section 2, Sections 18-26, Sections 35 and 38-43, and Section 44(1) and (3), with the remaining Act provisions scheduled one year or eighteen months later. The Digital Personal Data Protection Rules, 2025 were finally notified by G.S.R. 846(E) with the same staged logic.
- [LIMIT] Supreme Court/PIL institutional detail belongs to Polity 18; Emergency architecture to Polity 14; amendment/basic structure to Polity 10; FR-DPSP reconciliation to Polity 08; and policy evaluation of reservations to Social Justice. This package retains the constitutional mechanism needed for Fundamental Rights answers.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 1 — PART III AS AN ARCHITECTURE: RIGHTS, LIMITS, REMEDIES AND SPECIAL CONTROLS

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Part III is a constitutional operating system, not a bare catalogue: Articles 12-13 identify bound power and invalid law, Articles 14-30 secure six present rights, Article 32 supplies the remedy, and Articles 31A-35 plus 358-359 control exceptional protection or enforcement.

**Technical definition:** Articles 12-13 and 31A-35 are in Part III, but they are not separately counted among the six present Fundamental Rights.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Part III is a constitutional operating system, not a bare catalogue: Articles 12-13 identify bound power and invalid law, Articles 14-30 secure six present rights, Article 32 supplies the remedy, and Articles 31A-35 plus 358-359 control exceptional protection or enforcement.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Part Iii As An Architecture**
- **Rights**
- **Limits**
- **Remedies**
- **Special Controls**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Part Iii As An Architecture; define Rights, connect Limits with Remedies to explain the mechanism, and use Special Controls for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARCHITECTURE):** Part III is a constitutional operating system, not a bare catalogue: Articles 12-13 identify bound power and invalid law, Articles 14-30 secure six present rights, Article 32 supplies the remedy, and Articles 31A-35 plus 358-359 control exceptional protection or enforcement.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Part III is a connected design: Articles 12-13 open the gate, rights occupy Articles 14-30, Article 32 enforces them, and special clauses discipline immunity or suspension.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/01_part_iii_architecture.png)

[FACT] Part III is not merely a list of entitlements. It has four interacting elements:

1. **Gateway:** Article 12 identifies the State; Article 13 invalidates inconsistent law.
2. **Substantive rights:** Articles 14-30 protect equality, freedom, labour dignity, conscience and cultural autonomy.
3. **Enforcement:** Article 32 guarantees access to the Supreme Court for Fundamental Rights; Article 226 supplies wider High Court writ power.
4. **Constitutional controls:** Articles 31A-31C, 33-35 and 358-359 define limited immunity, special legislation or Emergency consequences.

[ANALYSIS] The correct exam method is therefore:

> identify the right → identify its beneficiary and respondent → locate the exact permitted restriction → apply the judicial test → choose the remedy → state any saving or Emergency caveat.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Six rights and their Article spans

| Right | Articles | Core exam question |
|---|---:|---|
| Equality | 14-18 | Is the distinction rational, non-arbitrary and compatible with substantive equality? |
| Freedom | 19-22 | Which freedom/liberty is engaged, and does the limit fit the exact constitutional clause? |
| Against exploitation | 23-24 | Does coercion or prohibited child labour exist, including through private action? |
| Freedom of religion | 25-28 | Is the claim religious, denominational or secular, and which textual limit applies? |
| Cultural and educational rights | 29-30 | Is the claimant a section of citizens or a religious/linguistic minority institution? |
| Constitutional remedies | 32 | Which writ and which court can cure the constitutional injury? |

> **UPSC trap:** Articles 12-13 and 31A-35 are in Part III, but they are not separately counted among the six present Fundamental Rights.

**Technical definition:** Positive duty: equal protection may require protection from private violence; Article 21 can require legal aid, custodial safeguards and fair investigation; Article 17 requires abolition backed by penal law.

- **Vertical**

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Beneficiary language and the respondent axis are separate. A right may protect all persons yet remain mainly vertical, or may be citizen-specific yet reach private discrimination.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/02_beneficiary_horizontality_matrix.png)

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Citizen-only and person-protective guarantees

| Provision | Textual beneficiary | Exam-safe formulation |
|---|---|---|
| Article 14 | any person | Citizens, non-citizens and juristic persons may invoke it where applicable. |
| Article 15 | citizen / citizens | Citizen-specific; Article 15(2) also reaches specified private exclusion. |
| Article 16 | all citizens | Citizen-specific equality in public employment. |
| Article 19 | all citizens | The six freedoms are citizen-only. Companies do not become citizens, though shareholder/media claims may be framed through affected citizens. |
| Articles 20-22 | person / no person | Protect persons, subject to Article 22's enemy-alien and preventive-detention distinctions. |
| Articles 23-24 | person-oriented prohibitions | Reach State and private exploitation. |
| Articles 25-28 | all persons / denominations / institutional rules | Religious freedom is not citizen-only. |
| Article 29 | section of citizens / no citizen | Textually citizen-linked. |
| Article 30 | all minorities | The clause does not use the word citizen; standard exam lists treat it as minority/citizen-linked, but quote the text accurately. |

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Vertical and horizontal application

- **Vertical:** the usual model is individual versus State. Articles 14, 16 and 19 primarily discipline State action.
- **Express horizontal reach:** Article 15(2) covers denial of access to specified public-facing places; Article 17 abolishes untouchability; Articles 23-24 prohibit exploitation by private actors too.
- **Remedial reach:** habeas corpus may issue against private detention; High Courts may use Article 226 against a non-State body performing a public duty.
- [LIMIT] *Kaushal Kishor v. State of Uttar Pradesh* (2023) contains important reasoning on the operation of Articles 19 and 21 beyond the State. Do not convert it into the loose proposition that every Fundamental Right now applies fully against every private person.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Negative and positive dimensions

- **Negative duty:** the State must not censor speech outside Article 19(2), discriminate only on prohibited grounds, or deprive liberty through unfair procedure.
- **Positive duty:** equal protection may require protection from private violence; Article 21 can require legal aid, custodial safeguards and fair investigation; Article 17 requires abolition backed by penal law.
- [ANALYSIS] Modern rights adjudication therefore asks not only, "Did the State interfere?" but also, "Did the State reasonably protect the conditions in which the right can be exercised?"

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 3 — QUALIFIED RIGHTS, REASONABLE RESTRICTIONS AND PROPORTIONALITY

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** "Reasonable restriction" and proportionality are related but not interchangeable slogans.

**Technical definition:** Legality: Is the restriction authorised by valid law rather than unsupported executive preference?

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Indian Fundamental Rights are qualified but not defeasible at executive will: every restriction must rest on valid law, fit the exact constitutional ground, pursue a legitimate aim and remain rational, necessary, balanced and procedurally safeguarded.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Qualified Rights**
- **Reasonable Restrictions**
- **Proportionality**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **Legality**
- **Legitimate constitutional aim**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Qualified Rights; define Reasonable Restrictions, connect Proportionality with CONTENT CLASSIFICATION to explain the mechanism, and use Legality for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (QUALIFIED RIGHTS / PROPORTIONALITY):** Indian Fundamental Rights are qualified but not defeasible at executive will: every restriction must rest on valid law, fit the exact constitutional ground, pursue a legitimate aim and remain rational, necessary, balanced and procedurally safeguarded.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE MAINS

[FACT] Fundamental Rights are not absolute. Their qualification takes different forms:

- Article 14 permits reasonable classification but bars class legislation and arbitrariness.
- Article 19 contains six freedoms and four different restriction clauses; the grounds are closed, not illustrative.
- Articles 25-26 are expressly subject to public order, morality and health; Article 25 is also subject to other Fundamental Rights.
- Article 22 itself authorises preventive detention while imposing minimum safeguards.
- Articles 31A-31C save specified laws from specified challenges.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — The modern review sequence

1. **Legality:** Is the restriction authorised by valid law rather than unsupported executive preference?
2. **Legitimate constitutional aim:** Does it fit the exact provision, such as Article 19(2) public order?
3. **Rational connection:** Is the measure capable of advancing that aim?
4. **Necessity:** Is a less rights-restrictive but equally effective measure available?
5. **Balancing:** Is the rights cost excessive relative to the public benefit?
6. **Procedural safeguards:** Are reasons, publication, hearing, review and time limits present?

[ANALYSIS] "Reasonable restriction" and proportionality are related but not interchangeable slogans. Always begin with the Article's text; proportionality disciplines the chosen means.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Article 12 looks beyond legal form to governmental domination: an entity may be State when its financial, functional and administrative relationship reveals pervasive governmental control, while Article 226 may separately reach a non-State body performing a public duty.

**Technical definition:** Article 226 can reach a non-State body for the public-law element of its work.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Article 12 looks beyond legal form to governmental domination: an entity may be State when its financial, functional and administrative relationship reveals pervasive governmental control, while Article 226 may separately reach a non-State body performing a public duty.

- **Article 12 - The State**
- **The Instrumentality Inquiry**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **public duty**
- **Article 12**

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARTICLE 12):** Article 12 looks beyond legal form to governmental domination: an entity may be State when its financial, functional and administrative relationship reveals pervasive governmental control, while Article 226 may separately reach a non-State body performing a public duty.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Article 12 begins with express bodies and then asks whether an 'other authority' is functionally an instrumentality or agency of government.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/03_article_12_state_test.png)

[FACT] Article 12 includes:

- Government and Parliament of India;
- Government and Legislature of each State;
- all local authorities; and
- other authorities within India or under the control of the Government of India.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Other authorities

[FACT] *Rajasthan Electricity Board v. Mohan Lal* rejected an unduly narrow reading tied only to sovereign functions. *R.D. Shetty* and *Ajay Hasia* developed the instrumentality/agency approach.

Relevant indicators include:

- substantial government ownership or finance;
- deep and pervasive control;
- functions of public importance closely related to governmental functions;
- State-conferred monopoly;
- transfer of a government department; and
- the cumulative factual relationship rather than the body's corporate form.

[LIMIT] No single factor is conclusive. The later approach in *Pradeep Kumar Biswas* asks whether the body is financially, functionally and administratively dominated by or under government control of a pervasive character.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 12 versus Article 226

- A body may fail the Article 12 State test yet still perform a **public duty**.
- Article 226 can reach a non-State body for the public-law element of its work.
- [ANALYSIS] Therefore, "not State under Article 12" does not always mean "immune from writ jurisdiction."

> **UPSC trap:** registration as a society or company neither automatically includes nor excludes a body from Article 12.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 5 — ARTICLE 13 - LAW, JUDICIAL REVIEW AND THE VOIDNESS DOCTRINES

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Article 13(1): pre-Constitution laws are void to the extent of inconsistency with Fundamental Rights.

**Technical definition:** Article 13(2): the State shall not make law taking away or abridging Fundamental Rights; contravening law is void to the extent of contravention.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Article 13 converts rights into judicially enforceable limits by defining law broadly and using severability, eclipse and non-waiver, while constitutional amendments are reviewed through Article 368 and the basic-structure boundary.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Article 13 - Law**
- **Judicial Review**
- **The Voidness Doctrines**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **to the extent**
- **Test**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Article 13 - Law; define Judicial Review, connect The Voidness Doctrines with CONTENT CLASSIFICATION to explain the mechanism, and use to the extent for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARTICLE 13):** Article 13 converts rights into judicially enforceable limits by defining law broadly and using severability, eclipse and non-waiver, while constitutional amendments are reviewed through Article 368 and the basic-structure boundary.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Article 13 defines law broadly, invalidates inconsistency to its extent, and operates through severability, eclipse, non-waiver and carefully used prospective overruling.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/04_article_13_doctrine_tree.png)

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — What "law" includes

[FACT] Article 13(3)(a) includes an ordinance, order, bye-law, rule, regulation, notification, custom or usage having in the territory of India the force of law.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Pre- and post-Constitution law

- Article 13(1): pre-Constitution laws are void **to the extent** of inconsistency with Fundamental Rights.
- Article 13(2): the State shall not make law taking away or abridging Fundamental Rights; contravening law is void to the extent of contravention.
- Article 13(3): defines law and laws in force.
- Article 13(4): inserted by the Twenty-fourth Amendment; Article 13 does not apply to constitutional amendments under Article 368.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Doctrine of severability

[FACT] If the invalid provision can be separated from the valid remainder without defeating the legislative scheme, only the offending portion falls. The phrase "to the extent of" supports limited invalidation.

**Test:** Can the remainder operate independently, and would the legislature have enacted it without the invalid part?

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Doctrine of eclipse

[FACT] *Bhikaji Narain Dhakras* explains that a conflicting pre-Constitution law is not erased for all purposes; it lies dormant against the Fundamental Right and can revive if the constitutional shadow is removed.

[LIMIT] The classic eclipse rule chiefly concerns pre-Constitution law. A post-Constitution law made in direct violation of Article 13(2) is ordinarily void from inception to the extent of violation.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — No waiver of Fundamental Rights

[FACT] *Basheshar Nath v. Commissioner of Income Tax* held that an individual cannot waive a Fundamental Right so as to validate unconstitutional State action. *Olga Tellis* later rejected estoppel against a Fundamental Right.

[ANALYSIS] Rights are structural limits on public power, not merely private benefits that the holder may bargain away.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Prospective overruling

[FACT] *I.C. Golaknath v. State of Punjab* used prospective overruling: it announced a rule for the future without unsettling past amendments. *Kesavananda Bharati* displaced Golaknath's amendment theory but retained the Indian court's capacity to calibrate temporal effect where justified.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Constitutional amendments and basic structure

| Stage | Proposition |
|---|---|
| *Shankari Prasad* / *Sajjan Singh* | Constitutional amendment not ordinary "law" under Article 13. |
| *Golaknath* (1967) | Amendment treated as law; Parliament could not abridge FRs; applied prospectively. |
| Twenty-fourth Amendment | Expressly affirmed amendment power and added Article 13(4). |
| *Kesavananda Bharati* (1973) | Parliament may amend every Part, including FRs, but cannot damage basic structure. |
| *Minerva Mills* (1980) | Limited amending power and harmony between Parts III and IV are basic features. |

[LIMIT] The complete amendment doctrine belongs to Polity 10. Here the exam rule is: ordinary law is tested under Article 13; constitutional amendment is tested under the basic-structure limitation.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 6 — ARTICLE 14 - TWIN EQUALITY, CLASSIFICATION AND ANTI-ARBITRARINESS

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** classification tests the structure of differentiation; arbitrariness tests irrational, capricious or disproportionate State power even where a neat class is difficult to identify.

**Technical definition:** Intelligible differentia: the included group must be distinguishable from the excluded group.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> classification tests the structure of differentiation; arbitrariness tests irrational, capricious or disproportionate State power even where a neat class is difficult to identify.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Article 14 - Twin Equality**
- **Anti-Arbitrariness**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **class legislation**
- **Intelligible differentia**
- **Rational nexus**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Article 14 - Twin Equality; define Anti-Arbitrariness, connect CONTENT CLASSIFICATION with class legislation to explain the mechanism, and use Intelligible differentia for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARTICLE 14):** Article 14 combines equality before law with equal protection: it permits reasonable classification only through intelligible differentia and rational nexus, and independently invalidates arbitrary State action.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Articles 14-18 form a code against privilege, exclusion, caste hierarchy and artificial rank, while permitting carefully designed substantive equality.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/05_equality_code.png)

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Twin concepts

| Concept | Source | Nature | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| Equality before law | British rule-of-law tradition | negative | no special privilege; equal subjection to ordinary law |
| Equal protection of laws | US Fourteenth Amendment influence | positive | like cases treated alike; State may differentiate to correct relevant differences |

[FACT] Article 14 protects every person. It forbids **class legislation**, not all classification.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Reasonable classification test

1. **Intelligible differentia:** the included group must be distinguishable from the excluded group.
2. **Rational nexus:** the differentia must relate to the law's object.

[LIMIT] The classification cannot be artificial, evasive or unrelated. The object itself must be constitutionally legitimate.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — From classification to arbitrariness

- *E.P. Royappa* treated equality and arbitrariness as sworn enemies.
- *Maneka Gandhi* linked Articles 14, 19 and 21 and required non-arbitrary, fair procedure.
- *Ajay Hasia* reinforced that arbitrary State action violates Article 14.
- *Shayara Bano* used manifest arbitrariness in reviewing legislation.

[ANALYSIS] A high-scoring answer does not abandon classification for arbitrariness. It uses both:

> classification tests the structure of differentiation; arbitrariness tests irrational, capricious or disproportionate State power even where a neat class is difficult to identify.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Constitutional exceptions and immunities

- President and Governors receive specified immunities under Article 361.
- Legislative speech and votes receive privileges under Articles 105 and 194.
- Foreign sovereigns/diplomats and international organisations may possess recognised immunities.
- [LIMIT] These are constitutionally or legally structured immunities, not proof that rule of law is absent.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 7 — ARTICLES 15 AND 16 - NON-DISCRIMINATION AND PROTECTIVE DISCRIMINATION

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Articles 15(4) and 16(4) are not grudging exceptions to equality.

**Technical definition:** Article 15(4) followed State of Madras v.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Articles 15(4) and 16(4) are not grudging exceptions to equality.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Articles 15**
- **Protective Discrimination**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **only**
- **special provisions for women and children**
- **16 - Non-Discrimination**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Articles 15; define Protective Discrimination, connect CONTENT CLASSIFICATION with only to explain the mechanism, and use special provisions for women and children for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARTICLES 15-16):** Articles 15 and 16 move equality from non-discrimination to substantive opportunity by prohibiting exact grounds yet authorising carefully targeted special provisions and reservation through distinct enabling clauses.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 15

[FACT] Article 15(1) bars the State from discriminating against a citizen on grounds **only** of religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth or any of them.

[FACT] Article 15(2) prevents a citizen, on those grounds only, from being subjected to disability in access to:

- shops, public restaurants, hotels and places of public entertainment; and
- wells, tanks, bathing ghats, roads and places of public resort maintained wholly/partly from State funds or dedicated to public use.

This clause has direct horizontal force.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Enabling clauses under Article 15

| Clause | Constitutional purpose |
|---|---|
| 15(3) | special provisions for women and children |
| 15(4) | advancement of socially and educationally backward classes, SCs and STs |
| 15(5) | admission-related special provisions in educational institutions, including private aided/unaided institutions, excluding Article 30(1) minority institutions |
| 15(6) | EWS special provisions, including reservation up to 10 per cent, for classes outside 15(4)/(5) coverage |

[FACT] Article 15(4) followed *State of Madras v. Champakam Dorairajan* and the First Amendment. Article 15(5) came through the Ninety-third Amendment. Article 15(6) came through the 103rd Amendment.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 16

[FACT] Article 16(1) guarantees equality of opportunity to citizens in public employment. Article 16(2) bars discrimination on religion, race, caste, sex, descent, place of birth, residence or any of them.

| Clause | Function |
|---|---|
| 16(3) | Parliament may prescribe residence requirements for specified employment |
| 16(4) | enables reservation for a backward class inadequately represented in State services |
| 16(4A) | enables promotion reservation with consequential seniority for SCs/STs, subject to doctrine |
| 16(4B) | allows unfilled reserved vacancies to be treated as a separate class for carry-forward |
| 16(5) | permits religion-linked office requirements in a religious/denominational institution |
| 16(6) | enables EWS reservation up to 10 per cent outside existing reserved classes |

[ANALYSIS] Articles 15(4) and 16(4) are not grudging exceptions to equality. They are textual methods of substantive equality. Yet they remain enabling powers, not an individual Fundamental Right to demand reservation in every setting.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 8 — RESERVATION DOCTRINE: CREAMY LAYER, CEILING, PROMOTION, EFFICIENCY, EWS AND SC SUB-CLASSIFICATION

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Reservation is a technique of substantive equality, but its design remains evidence-based, reviewable and attentive to creamy-layer exclusion, representation, promotion conditions, backlog treatment, EWS text and administrative efficiency.

**Technical definition:** Efficiency is not a veto owned by the unreserved category.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Reservation is a technique of substantive equality, but its design remains evidence-based, reviewable and attentive to creamy-layer exclusion, representation, promotion conditions, backlog treatment, EWS text and administrative efficiency.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Reservation Doctrine**
- **Creamy Layer**
- **Ceiling**
- **Promotion**
- **Efficiency**
- **Ews**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Reservation Doctrine; define Creamy Layer, connect Ceiling with Promotion to explain the mechanism, and use Efficiency for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (RESERVATION CONTROL):** Reservation is a technique of substantive equality, but its design remains evidence-based, reviewable and attentive to creamy-layer exclusion, representation, promotion conditions, backlog treatment, EWS text and administrative efficiency.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

![Reservation doctrine is a timeline of constitutional amendments and judicial controls, culminating in the 2024 permission for evidence-based SC sub-classification.](upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Learner-v2-Refreshed/Polity/Subject-Wide-Syllabus/learning-sessions/polity-07/g15/assets/06_reservation_timeline.png)

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Indra Sawhney (1992)

[FACT] The nine-judge decision:

- upheld 27 per cent OBC reservation;
- required exclusion of the OBC creamy layer;
- treated the 50 per cent ceiling as the ordinary constitutional rule, with extraordinary situations narrowly conceived;
- held that Article 16(4) did not authorise reservation in promotion; and
- accepted sub-classification among backward classes where rationally designed.

[LIMIT] "Fifty per cent can never be crossed" is too absolute. The case described an ordinary ceiling with exceptional-space language; later constitutional amendments and EWS doctrine require precise treatment.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Promotion amendments and doctrine

- 77th Amendment: Article 16(4A), promotion reservation for SCs/STs.
- 81st Amendment: Article 16(4B), backlog vacancies as a separate class.
- 82nd Amendment: proviso to Article 335 permitting relaxation in qualifying marks/standards for SC/ST promotion reservation.
- 85th Amendment: consequential seniority.
- *M. Nagaraj* upheld the amendments but required constitutional discipline, including quantifiable data on inadequate representation and attention to Article 335.
- *Jarnail Singh* removed the requirement that the State prove backwardness of SCs/STs afresh, while retaining data-based inadequacy and efficiency controls.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Creamy layer

- [FACT] The settled Indra Sawhney creamy-layer rule directly concerns OBC reservation.
- [LIMIT] Do not automatically state that a single identical creamy-layer rule now governs every SC/ST reservation context. Concurring opinions and later debates must not be converted into a universal holding.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 335 and efficiency

[FACT] Claims of SCs/STs in services must be considered consistently with maintaining administrative efficiency. The proviso permits relaxation in qualifying marks or evaluation standards for specified promotion-reservation purposes.

[ANALYSIS] Efficiency is not a veto owned by the unreserved category. A constitutional answer should ask how representative administration, competence, training and fair standards interact.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — EWS

[FACT] The 103rd Amendment inserted Articles 15(6) and 16(6). In *Janhit Abhiyan v. Union of India* (2022), a majority upheld:

- economic criteria as a basis for the EWS class;
- exclusion of classes already covered by SC/ST/OBC reservation; and
- the additional 10 per cent design against a basic-structure challenge.

[LIMIT] The decision was by majority, not unanimity.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Davinder Singh (2024)

[CURRENT] A seven-judge Bench overruled the bar created by *E.V. Chinnaiah* and held that States may sub-classify Scheduled Castes for distributing reservation benefits more effectively.

Exam-safe propositions:

1. sub-classification is **permitted**, not constitutionally mandated;
2. it cannot alter the Presidential List under Article 341;
3. it must rest on evidence of differential disadvantage/representation rather than political assertion;
4. a State cannot reserve the entire benefit for one sub-class and exclude the rest; and
5. judicial review remains available.

[LIMIT] The judgment does not authorise a State to add or delete castes from the Scheduled Castes list.

#### SOURCE HEADING [3] — SESSION 9 — ARTICLES 17 AND 18 - ANTI-UNTOUCHABILITY AND ANTI-TITLE GUARANTEES

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Articles 17 and 18 attack inherited civic hierarchy horizontally and symbolically: untouchability is abolished and punishable, while titles are prohibited without invalidating military, academic or properly used national distinctions.

**Technical definition:** Article 17 is transformative: it does not merely restrain government classification; it constitutionally abolishes a social hierarchy and requires public enforcement.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Articles 17 and 18 attack inherited civic hierarchy horizontally and symbolically: untouchability is abolished and punishable, while titles are prohibited without invalidating military, academic or properly used national distinctions.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **Articles 17**
- **Anti-Title Guarantees**
- **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION**
- **Article 17**
- **Article 18**
- **18 - Anti-Untouchability**

**How to use them:** Frame the answer through Articles 17; define Anti-Title Guarantees, connect CONTENT CLASSIFICATION with Article 17 to explain the mechanism, and use Article 18 for the decisive comparison or qualification.

> **ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM (ARTICLES 17-18):** Articles 17 and 18 attack inherited civic hierarchy horizontally and symbolically: untouchability is abolished and punishable, while titles are prohibited without invalidating military, academic or properly used national distinctions.

> **CONTENT CLASSIFICATION:** CORE PRELIMS + CORE MAINS

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 17

[FACT] Untouchability is abolished; its practice in any form is forbidden; enforcement of any disability arising from it is an offence punishable by law.

- It is absolute in text and operates against private persons.
- The Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955 penalises untouchability disabilities.
- The Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989 addresses specified caste atrocities.
- [LIMIT] "Untouchability" is understood in its historical caste-based constitutional sense, not every form of social avoidance.

[ANALYSIS] Article 17 is transformative: it does not merely restrain government classification; it constitutionally abolishes a social hierarchy and requires public enforcement.

#### SOURCE HEADING [4] — Article 18

| Clause | Rule |
|---|---|
| 18(1) | State shall not confer titles except military or academic distinctions |
| 18(2) | citizen shall not accept a title from a foreign State |
| 18(3) | non-citizen holding office of profit/trust under State requires President's consent to accept foreign title |
| 18(4) | office-holder requires President's consent for present, emolument or office from foreign State |

[FACT] *Balaji Raghavan v. Union of India* (1996) held that Bharat Ratna and Padma awards are not prohibited titles, but they cannot be used as prefixes or suffixes.

> **UPSC trap:** Article 17 belongs to the Right to Equality, not the Right against Exploitation.
